At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Like on calls with suppliers, I’ll think I’m sitting still, but then I notice my foot is bouncing under the desk the whole time. And I’m clicking a pen, or twisting my ring around and around—sometimes so much I have to mute myself because the pen noise is probably annoying, or I move my hands out of the camera view. I don’t even always realize I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often worry,” exactly. I mean, I think about work a lot because there’s always something—orders, prices changing, someone needing an answer yesterday. And finances, sure, I check bills and all that, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nRelationships… I mean, I’ll suddenly remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then I feel bad for a minute, but then something else comes up. So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. I mean, there are situations I don’t love—like those big vendor networking breakfasts where everyone’s balancing coffee and trying to remember names, and the muffins are always weirdly dry—but I’ll still go if I have to. I might put it off a little if something feels awkward, but I don’t think it’s because I’m scared or anything. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the way I think you mean. I’ll get a song stuck in my head, or I’ll keep circling back to something I need to order for work, like “don’t forget the seasonal display stuff,” and then I’ll remember I also need to text my sister, and—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nI don’t think I have thoughts that keep coming back and bother me even when I’m trying not to think about them. Or at least I haven’t noticed that as a big thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I guess what brings me in is this constant restless feeling. Like I’m wound too tight and I can’t ever fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting at work, or painting, or even doing yoga—which is ridiculous, because that’s supposed to be calming. I’ll be there trying to relax and my body just feels like it wants to jump out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… um, I’m not sure. I mean, I think about work stuff a lot, like pricing or what needs ordering, and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to text my sister back, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. More like my mind just hops around and I’m following it before I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Like, not rituals exactly. I mean, I do have little habits, like checking my bag for my keys before I leave, but I think everyone does that—actually my coworker once lost her keys in a shipment box, which was a whole thing—but no, I don’t feel like I have to do some specific routine to make anxiety go away. Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. Like, not rituals exactly. I mean, I do have little habits, like checking my bag for my keys before I leave, but I think everyone does that—actually my coworker once lost her keys in a shipment box, which was a whole thing—but no, I don’t feel like I have to do some specific routine to make anxiety go away. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.

More like my mind just hops around and I’m following it before I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Like, not rituals exactly. I mean, I do have little habits, like checking my bag for my keys before I leave, but I think everyone does that—actually my coworker once lost her keys in a shipment box, which was a whole thing—but no, I don’t feel like I have to do some specific routine to make anxiety go away. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Like on calls with suppliers, I’ll think I’m sitting still, but then I notice my foot is bouncing under the desk the whole time. I don’t even always realize I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often worry,” exactly. I mean, I think about work a lot because there’s always something—orders, prices changing, someone needing an answer yesterday. And finances, sure, I check bills and all that, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nRelationships… I mean, I’ll suddenly remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then I feel bad for a minute, but then something else comes up. So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.

It’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.

So yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.

I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 51 / wholesale_or_retail_buyer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work it has led to corrected purchase orders and extra review from my planning coordinator, and at home it has made family dinners more tense because people feel talked over.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.

And at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, there are moments, sure—sometimes painting can pull me in for a little while, depending on the day, and yoga is supposed to do that, though honestly sometimes I’m just thinking about whether I’m doing the pose right or how long we’re staying there.

But extended period? Not really. What brings me in is more that constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting in a planning meeting or doing something relaxing. It’s like my body doesn’t quite believe we’re allowed to sit still.

I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I guess what brings me in is this constant restless feeling. Like I’m wound too tight and I can’t ever fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting at work, or painting, or even doing yoga—which is ridiculous, because that’s supposed to be calming. I’ll be there trying to relax and my body just feels like it wants to jump out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… um, I’m not sure. I mean, I think about work stuff a lot, like pricing or what needs ordering, and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to text my sister back, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like one thought stuck on a loop exactly. More like my mind just hops around and I’m following it before I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? Like, not rituals exactly. I mean, I do have little habits, like checking my bag for my keys before I leave, but I think everyone does that—actually my coworker once lost her keys in a shipment box, which was a whole thing—but no, I don’t feel like I have to do some specific routine to make anxiety go away. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Like on calls with suppliers, I’ll think I’m sitting still, but then I notice my foot is bouncing under the desk the whole time. And I’m clicking a pen, or twisting my ring around and around—sometimes so much I have to mute myself because the pen noise is probably annoying, or I move my hands out of the camera view. I don’t even always realize I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often worry,” exactly. I mean, I think about work a lot because there’s always something—orders, prices changing, someone needing an answer yesterday. And finances, sure, I check bills and all that, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nRelationships… I mean, I’ll suddenly remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then I feel bad for a minute, but then something else comes up. So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. I mean, there are situations I don’t love—like those big vendor networking breakfasts where everyone’s balancing coffee and trying to remember names, and the muffins are always weirdly dry—but I’ll still go if I have to. I might put it off a little if something feels awkward, but I don’t think it’s because I’m scared or anything. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the way I think you mean. I’ll get a song stuck in my head, or I’ll keep circling back to something I need to order for work, like “don’t forget the seasonal display stuff,” and then I’ll remember I also need to text my sister, and—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nI don’t think I have thoughts that keep coming back and bother me even when I’m trying not to think about them. Or at least I haven’t noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry about work, finances, or relationships is more than what others would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, compared to other people? That’s hard to judge. In retail, everybody seems stressed about something—shipping delays, budgets, who approved what display, that kind of thing.\n\nI don’t think I’m more worried than they would be, though. I might talk about it more in the moment, or bounce around between things I need to handle, but I don’t feel like I’m carrying some huge worry that’s out of proportion. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… hmm. I don’t think so? I mean, I’ll sometimes recheck whether I sent an email, but that’s usually because I’ve got, like, six tabs open and someone asking me about pricing at the same time. Or I’ll straighten a shelf display if I walk by and it looks wrong, but that’s kind of work habit, not like I have to do it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions that feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Intrusive or unwanted… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, I guess what brings me in is this constant restless feeling. Like I’m wound too tight and I can’t ever fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting at work, or painting, or even doing yoga—which is ridiculous, because that’s supposed to be calming. I’ll be there trying to relax and my body just feels like it wants to jump out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Repetitive thoughts… um, I’m not sure. I mean, I think about work stuff a lot, like pricing or what needs ordering, and then suddenly I’m remembering I forgot to text my sister back, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like one thought stuck on a loop exactly. More like my mind just hops around and I’m following it before I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, I do have little habits, like checking my bag for my keys before I leave, but I think everyone does that—actually my coworker once lost her keys in a shipment box, which was a whole thing—but no, I don’t feel like I have to do some specific routine to make anxiety go away. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Like on calls with suppliers, I’ll think I’m sitting still, but then I notice my foot is bouncing under the desk the whole time. And I’m clicking a pen, or twisting my ring around and around—sometimes so much I have to mute myself because the pen noise is probably annoying, or I move my hands out of the camera view. I don’t even always realize I’m doing it at first.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know if I’d say “often worry,” exactly. I mean, I think about work a lot because there’s always something—orders, prices changing, someone needing an answer yesterday. And finances, sure, I check bills and all that, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nRelationships… I mean, I’ll suddenly remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then I feel bad for a minute, but then something else comes up. So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. I mean, there are situations I don’t love—like those big vendor networking breakfasts where everyone’s balancing coffee and trying to remember names, and the muffins are always weirdly dry—but I’ll still go if I have to. I might put it off a little if something feels awkward, but I don’t think it’s because I’m scared or anything. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the way I think you mean. I’ll get a song stuck in my head, or I’ll keep circling back to something I need to order for work, like “don’t forget the seasonal display stuff,” and then I’ll remember I also need to text my sister, and—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nI don’t think I have thoughts that keep coming back and bother me even when I’m trying not to think about them. Or at least I haven’t noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry about work, finances, or relationships is more than what others would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, compared to other people? That’s hard to judge. In retail, everybody seems stressed about something—shipping delays, budgets, who approved what display, that kind of thing.\n\nI don’t think I’m more worried than they would be, though. I might talk about it more in the moment, or bounce around between things I need to handle, but I don’t feel like I’m carrying some huge worry that’s out of proportion. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… hmm. I don’t think so? I mean, I’ll sometimes recheck whether I sent an email, but that’s usually because I’ve got, like, six tabs open and someone asking me about pricing at the same time. Or I’ll straighten a shelf display if I walk by and it looks wrong, but that’s kind of work habit, not like I have to do it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions that feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Intrusive or unwanted… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt completely calm and focused for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, there are moments, sure—sometimes painting can pull me in for a little while, depending on the day, and yoga is supposed to do that, though honestly sometimes I’m just thinking about whether I’m doing the pose right or how long we’re staying there.\n\nBut extended period? Not really. What brings me in is more that constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting in a planning meeting or doing something relaxing. It’s like my body doesn’t quite believe we’re allowed to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were able to focus very intensely on a single task for a long period of time when it really interested you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as a long period of time, exactly. I mean, if something really grabs me, I can get into it for a bit—like, I’ll start comparing vendors or looking at colors for a display and suddenly I’m down some little rabbit hole… but then I’ll also remember the laundry, or an email, or I’ll get up to refill my coffee and somehow start wiping the counter.\n\nSo… yes and no? I don’t know if that counts as “very intensely.” I can be interested in something and still kind of bounce around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or failing to finish tasks because you get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. Mostly at work, with vendor spreadsheets and purchase orders. I think the restless part bothers me more, honestly. The mistakes are embarrassing, especially at work, but the restless feeling is more… constant. Like I’m wound too tight and can’t quite settle down.

Even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting—or even doing something that should be relaxing, like painting or yoga—I still feel like I’m buzzing under the surface. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like my body is always trying to go somewhere else.

In retail, everybody seems stressed about something—shipping delays, budgets, who approved what display, that kind of thing.\n\nI don’t think I’m more worried than they would be, though. I might talk about it more in the moment, or bounce around between things I need to handle, but I don’t feel like I’m carrying some huge worry that’s out of proportion. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… hmm. I don’t think so? I mean, I’ll sometimes recheck whether I sent an email, but that’s usually because I’ve got, like, six tabs open and someone asking me about pricing at the same time. Or I’ll straighten a shelf display if I walk by and it looks wrong, but that’s kind of work habit, not like I have to do it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions that feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Intrusive or unwanted… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt completely calm and focused for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, there are moments, sure—sometimes painting can pull me in for a little while, depending on the day, and yoga is supposed to do that, though honestly sometimes I’m just thinking about whether I’m doing the pose right or how long we’re staying there.\n\nBut extended period? Not really. What brings me in is more that constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting in a planning meeting or doing something relaxing. It’s like my body doesn’t quite believe we’re allowed to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were able to focus very intensely on a single task for a long period of time when it really interested you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as a long period of time, exactly. I mean, if something really grabs me, I can get into it for a bit—like, I’ll start comparing vendors or looking at colors for a display and suddenly I’m down some little rabbit hole… but then I’ll also remember the laundry, or an email, or I’ll get up to refill my coffee and somehow start wiping the counter.\n\nSo… yes and no? I don’t know if that counts as “very intensely.” I can be interested in something and still kind of bounce around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or failing to finish tasks because you get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. Mostly at work, with vendor spreadsheets and purchase orders. I’ll think I checked something carefully—like really checked it—and then my planning coordinator will catch that I transposed a SKU, or missed a freight surcharge, or carried the wrong quantity over into the purchase order.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’ll be looking right at it, and then… I don’t know, my attention sort of slips to the next thing. And spreadsheets are already so easy to glaze over, all the rows start looking the same after a while. So yes, over the past couple of years that’s been a recurring thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty sitting still or feeling restless is more bothersome to you than your tendency to make careless mistakes or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I think the restless part bothers me more, honestly. The mistakes are embarrassing, especially at work, but the restless feeling is more… constant. Like I’m wound too tight and can’t quite settle down.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting—or even doing something that should be relaxing, like painting or yoga—I still feel like I’m buzzing under the surface. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like my body is always trying to go somewhere else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven’t been diagnosed with it, no. I’ve wondered a little, I guess, but then I also wonder if I’m just busy or if this is how I’ve always been.

I mean, my job has so many moving parts—orders, vendors, timelines—and sometimes I think anyone would feel scattered doing it. But then I talk to other people and they seem to handle it more smoothly, and I start thinking, “Okay, maybe this is something.” I’m not really sure.

More like my mind just hops around and I’m following it before I realize I’ve gone somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. I might put it off a little if something feels awkward, but I don’t think it’s because I’m scared or anything. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the way I think you mean. I’ll get a song stuck in my head, or I’ll keep circling back to something I need to order for work, like “don’t forget the seasonal display stuff,” and then I’ll remember I also need to text my sister, and—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nI don’t think I have thoughts that keep coming back and bother me even when I’m trying not to think about them. Or at least I haven’t noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry about work, finances, or relationships is more than what others would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. In retail, everybody seems stressed about something—shipping delays, budgets, who approved what display, that kind of thing.\n\nI don’t think I’m more worried than they would be, though. I might talk about it more in the moment, or bounce around between things I need to handle, but I don’t feel like I’m carrying some huge worry that’s out of proportion. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… hmm. I don’t think so? I mean, I’ll sometimes recheck whether I sent an email, but that’s usually because I’ve got, like, six tabs open and someone asking me about pricing at the same time. Or I’ll straighten a shelf display if I walk by and it looks wrong, but that’s kind of work habit, not like I have to do it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions that feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Intrusive or unwanted… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt completely calm and focused for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt completely calm and focused for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, there are moments, sure—sometimes painting can pull me in for a little while, depending on the day, and yoga is supposed to do that, though honestly sometimes I’m just thinking about whether I’m doing the pose right or how long we’re staying there.\n\nBut extended period? Not really. What brings me in is more that constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting in a planning meeting or doing something relaxing. It’s like my body doesn’t quite believe we’re allowed to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were able to focus very intensely on a single task for a long period of time when it really interested you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as a long period of time, exactly. I mean, if something really grabs me, I can get into it for a bit—like, I’ll start comparing vendors or looking at colors for a display and suddenly I’m down some little rabbit hole… but then I’ll also remember the laundry, or an email, or I’ll get up to refill my coffee and somehow start wiping the counter.\n\nSo… yes and no? I don’t know if that counts as “very intensely.” I can be interested in something and still kind of bounce around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or failing to finish tasks because you get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. Mostly at work, with vendor spreadsheets and purchase orders. And finances, sure, I check bills and all that, but I don’t feel like I’m sitting there worrying all day.\n\nRelationships… I mean, I’ll suddenly remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then I feel bad for a minute, but then something else comes up. So… maybe normal worry? I’m not totally sure what counts as too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid certain situations or activities because they make you feel anxious or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I avoid things because I’m anxious, no. I mean, there are situations I don’t love—like those big vendor networking breakfasts where everyone’s balancing coffee and trying to remember names, and the muffins are always weirdly dry—but I’ll still go if I have to. I might put it off a little if something feels awkward, but I don’t think it’s because I’m scared or anything. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep coming back to you, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not in the way I think you mean. I’ll get a song stuck in my head, or I’ll keep circling back to something I need to order for work, like “don’t forget the seasonal display stuff,” and then I’ll remember I also need to text my sister, and—sorry, that’s not really the question.\n\nI don’t think I have thoughts that keep coming back and bother me even when I’m trying not to think about them. Or at least I haven’t noticed that as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worry about work, finances, or relationships is more than what others would experience in the same situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, compared to other people? That’s hard to judge. In retail, everybody seems stressed about something—shipping delays, budgets, who approved what display, that kind of thing.\n\nI don’t think I’m more worried than they would be, though. I might talk about it more in the moment, or bounce around between things I need to handle, but I don’t feel like I’m carrying some huge worry that’s out of proportion. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: Overwhelming urge… hmm. I don’t think so? I mean, I’ll sometimes recheck whether I sent an email, but that’s usually because I’ve got, like, six tabs open and someone asking me about pricing at the same time. Or I’ll straighten a shelf display if I walk by and it looks wrong, but that’s kind of work habit, not like I have to do it over and over or something bad will happen.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed any repeating actions that feel overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that feel intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: Intrusive or unwanted… I don’t think so, no. I mean, I’ll have random thoughts pop in, like remembering I forgot to compare two vendors or wondering if I left my coffee in the car—which I do more often than I should—but they don’t feel scary or like they don’t belong in my head.\n\nSo I guess… no, not really. Nothing that feels intrusive in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are your restless feelings and trouble sitting still interfering with your daily life or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not huge huge, but it has gotten in the way a bit. At work, if I’m kind of up and moving around and jumping between things, I’ll rush through a purchase order or not sit with it long enough, and then there have been corrected purchase orders. My planning coordinator has started giving them an extra look, which is a little embarrassing because she’s very organized—like, color-coded calendar organized, which I always admire and also don’t understand how people keep up with that.\n\nAnd at home, it’s more… dinners, I guess. I don’t always sit calmly through the whole thing, or I jump in when people are talking, and then my family feels talked over. That makes dinners more tense sometimes. So yes, significant maybe in a mild way, but enough that other people notice.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this constant restless feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s been… noticeably harder to manage for about two years now—actually a little over that, maybe two years and a couple months.\n\nIt sort of lined up with when the in-law conflicts started taking over more of our evenings. Not that I want to blame everything on that, because family stuff is always messy and everyone has their version of the story, but that’s when I remember feeling like I just couldn’t settle down as easily. Like evenings stopped feeling like downtime, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restless feelings and trouble sitting still are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it is distressing… not like panic-level distress, but it wears on me. What brings me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle.\n\nIt’s especially frustrating when I’m supposed to be doing something calm—like sitting through a planning meeting, or even painting, which I used to think of as relaxing. Or yoga, which is funny because the whole point is to calm down, and instead I’m thinking, okay, how much longer are we holding this pose? Did I answer that email? My mat is crooked. You know, just… not settling.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me. It makes me feel like I can’t really relax even when I’m trying to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships or at work because of your restlessness or difficulty sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, I have. At work it’s shown up with purchase orders—like I’ll move too quickly or not stay with the details long enough, and then they have to be corrected. My planning coordinator has started looking them over more closely, which is… honestly a little embarrassing.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I don’t always just sit and let the conversation breathe. I’ll jump in, or people feel like I’m talking over them. Then suddenly dinner feels less like dinner and more like everyone’s annoyed, and I’m thinking, “How did we get here?” So yes, it’s caused some problems, even if they’re not catastrophic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt completely calm and focused for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a long stretch where I felt completely calm and focused, no. I mean, there are moments, sure—sometimes painting can pull me in for a little while, depending on the day, and yoga is supposed to do that, though honestly sometimes I’m just thinking about whether I’m doing the pose right or how long we’re staying there.\n\nBut extended period? Not really. What brings me in is more that constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle, even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting in a planning meeting or doing something relaxing. It’s like my body doesn’t quite believe we’re allowed to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were able to focus very intensely on a single task for a long period of time when it really interested you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it as a long period of time, exactly. I mean, if something really grabs me, I can get into it for a bit—like, I’ll start comparing vendors or looking at colors for a display and suddenly I’m down some little rabbit hole… but then I’ll also remember the laundry, or an email, or I’ll get up to refill my coffee and somehow start wiping the counter.\n\nSo… yes and no? I don’t know if that counts as “very intensely.” I can be interested in something and still kind of bounce around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or failing to finish tasks because you get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that does happen. Mostly at work, with vendor spreadsheets and purchase orders. I’ll think I checked something carefully—like really checked it—and then my planning coordinator will catch that I transposed a SKU, or missed a freight surcharge, or carried the wrong quantity over into the purchase order.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’ll be looking right at it, and then… I don’t know, my attention sort of slips to the next thing. And spreadsheets are already so easy to glaze over, all the rows start looking the same after a while. So yes, over the past couple of years that’s been a recurring thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty sitting still or feeling restless is more bothersome to you than your tendency to make careless mistakes or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I think the restless part bothers me more, honestly. The mistakes are embarrassing, especially at work, but the restless feeling is more… constant. Like I’m wound too tight and can’t quite settle down.\n\nEven when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting—or even doing something that should be relaxing, like painting or yoga—I still feel like I’m buzzing under the surface. It’s hard to explain, but it feels like my body is always trying to go somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder or do you think you might have it?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been diagnosed with it, no. I’ve wondered a little, I guess, but then I also wonder if I’m just busy or if this is how I’ve always been.\n\nI mean, my job has so many moving parts—orders, vendors, timelines—and sometimes I think anyone would feel scattered doing it. But then I talk to other people and they seem to handle it more smoothly, and I start thinking, “Okay, maybe this is something.” I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just having a lot going on. I mean, sometimes I’ll start a task and then another email or call pulls me away, but I don’t know if that’s me losing focus or just the nature of the job.\n\nI do usually circle back eventually, I think. Though sometimes I’ll find a half-finished note or an open tab and think, “Oh right, I meant to do that.” But… I’m not sure I’d say I often don’t follow through on instructions. I’d probably need an example of what you mean."
